<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Spratly_Island" title="Spratly Island">Spratly Island</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Spratly" redirects here. For the surname, see <a href="/wiki/Spratly_(surname)" title="Spratly (surname)">Spratly (surname)</a>.</div>

<p>The <b>Spratly Islands</b><sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> are a <a href="/wiki/Spratly_Islands_dispute" title="Spratly Islands dispute">disputed archipelago</a> in the <a href="/wiki/South_China_Sea" title="South China Sea">South China Sea</a>. Composed of islands, <a href="/wiki/Islet" title="Islet">islets</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cay" title="Cay">cays</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Madrolle1939_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Madrolle1939-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> and more than 100 <a href="/wiki/Reef" title="Reef">reefs</a>, sometimes grouped in submerged old <a href="/wiki/Atoll" title="Atoll">atolls</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> the archipelago lies off the coasts of the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malaysia" title="Malaysia">Malaysia</a>, and southern <a href="/wiki/Vietnam" title="Vietnam">Vietnam</a>. Named after the 19th-century British whaling captain <a href="/wiki/Richard_Spratly" title="Richard Spratly">Richard Spratly</a> who sighted <a href="/wiki/Spratly_Island" title="Spratly Island">Spratly Island</a> in 1843, the islands contain less than 2&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (490 acres) of naturally occurring land area, which is spread over an area of more than 425,000&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (164,000&#160;sq&#160;mi).
</p><p>The Spratly Islands are one of the major archipelagos in the South China Sea which complicate governance and economics in this part of <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asia</a> due to their location in strategic shipping lanes. The islands are largely uninhabited, but offer rich fishing grounds and may contain significant oil and natural gas reserves,<sup id="cite_ref-Owen2012a_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owen2012a-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-bbc20161712_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc20161712-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and as such are important to the claimants in their attempts to establish international boundaries. Some of the islands have civilian settlements, but of the approximately 45 islands, cays, reefs and shoals that are occupied, all contain structures that are occupied by military forces from Malaysia, China (<a href="/wiki/China" title="China">PRC</a> and <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">ROC</a>), the Philippines or Vietnam. Additionally, <a href="/wiki/Brunei" title="Brunei">Brunei</a> has claimed an <a href="/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone" title="Exclusive economic zone">exclusive economic zone</a> in the southeastern part of the Spratly Islands, which includes the uninhabited <a href="/wiki/Louisa_Reef" title="Louisa Reef">Louisa Reef</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geographic_and_economic_overview">Geographic and economic overview</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/List_of_maritime_features_in_the_Spratly_Islands" title="List of maritime features in the Spratly Islands">List of maritime features in the Spratly Islands</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_(2015).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_%282015%29.jpg/250px-Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_%282015%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_%282015%29.jpg/375px-Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_%282015%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_%282015%29.jpg/500px-Spratly_Islands_in_the_South_China_Sea_Department_of_State_map_2016587286_%282015%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="17014" data-file-height="13147" /></a><figcaption>The Spratly Islands</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1939, the Spratly Islands were coral islets mostly inhabited by seabirds.<sup id="cite_ref-Madrolle1939_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Madrolle1939-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Despite the Spratly Islands naturally consisting of 19 islands <i>(see below)</i>, according to a Chinese 1986 source, the Spratly Islands consist of 14 islands or islets, 6 banks, 113 submerged reefs, 35 underwater banks and 21 underwater shoals.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The northeast part of the Spratly Islands is known as <a href="/wiki/Dangerous_Ground_(South_China_Sea)" title="Dangerous Ground (South China Sea)">Dangerous Ground</a> and is characterised by many low islands, sunken reefs, and degraded, sunken atolls with <a href="/wiki/Coral" title="Coral">coral</a> often rising abruptly from ocean depths greater than 1,000 metres (3,300&#160;ft) – all of which makes the area dangerous for navigation.
</p><p>The islands are all of similar nature; they are <a href="/wiki/Cay" title="Cay">cays</a> (or keys): sand islands formed on old degraded and submerged coral reefs.
</p><p>The Spratly Islands contain almost no <a href="/wiki/Arable_land" title="Arable land">arable land</a>, are largely uninhabited, and very few of the islands have a permanent drinkable water supply.<sup id="cite_ref-bbc20161712_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc20161712-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Natural resources include fish, <a href="/wiki/Guano" title="Guano">guano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">oil</a> and natural gas.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Economic activity has included <a href="/wiki/Commercial_fishing" title="Commercial fishing">commercial fishing</a>, shipping, guano mining, oil and gas exploitation, and more recently, tourism. The Spratly Islands are located near several primary shipping lanes.
</p><p>The islands and cays, listed in descending order of naturally occurring area, are:
</p>

<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mapspratly.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Mapspratly.jpg/220px-Mapspratly.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Mapspratly.jpg/330px-Mapspratly.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Mapspratly.jpg/440px-Mapspratly.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1014" data-file-height="1204" /></a><figcaption>A geographic map of Spratly Islands<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geology">Geology</span></h2>
<p>The Spratly Islands consist of islands, reefs, banks and shoals made up of biogenic <a href="/wiki/Carbonate_rock" title="Carbonate rock">carbonate</a>. These accumulations of biogenic carbonate lie upon the higher crests of major submarine ridges that are uplifted <a href="/wiki/Fault_block" title="Fault block">fault blocks</a> known by geologists as <a href="/wiki/Horst_(geology)" title="Horst (geology)">horsts</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> These horsts are part of a series of half-grabens and rotated fault-blocks which lie parallel and en echelon. The long axes of the horsts, rotated fault blocks and half-<a href="/wiki/Grabens" class="mw-redirect" title="Grabens">grabens</a> form well-defined linear trends that lie parallel to <a href="/wiki/Magnetic_anomaly" title="Magnetic anomaly">magnetic anomalies</a> exhibited by the <a href="/wiki/Oceanic_crust" title="Oceanic crust">oceanic crust</a> of the adjacent South China Sea. The horsts, rotated fault blocks, and the rock forming the bottoms of associated grabens consist of stretched and subsided <a href="/wiki/Continental_crust" title="Continental crust">continental crust</a> that is composed of <a href="/wiki/Triassic" title="Triassic">Triassic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jurassic" title="Jurassic">Jurassic</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cretaceous" title="Cretaceous">Cretaceous</a> <a href="/wiki/Strata" class="mw-redirect" title="Strata">strata</a> that include <a href="/wiki/Calc-alkaline_magma_series" title="Calc-alkaline magma series">calc-alkalic</a> <a href="/wiki/Extrusive" class="mw-redirect" title="Extrusive">extrusive</a> <a href="/wiki/Igneous" class="mw-redirect" title="Igneous">igneous</a> rocks, intermediate to acid <a href="/wiki/Intrusion" class="mw-redirect" title="Intrusion">intrusive</a> igneous rocks, <a href="/wiki/Sandstone" title="Sandstone">sandstones</a>, <a href="/wiki/Siltstone" title="Siltstone">siltstones</a>, dark-green <a href="/wiki/Claystone" class="mw-redirect" title="Claystone">claystones</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Metamorphic_rocks" class="mw-redirect" title="Metamorphic rocks">metamorphic rocks</a> that include <a href="/wiki/Biotite" title="Biotite">biotite</a>–<a href="/wiki/Muscovite" title="Muscovite">muscovite</a>–<a href="/wiki/Feldspar" title="Feldspar">feldspar</a>–<a href="/wiki/Quartz" title="Quartz">quartz</a> <a href="/wiki/Migmatites" class="mw-redirect" title="Migmatites">migmatites</a> and <a href="/wiki/Garnet" title="Garnet">garnet</a>–<a href="/wiki/Mica" title="Mica">mica</a> <a href="/wiki/Schists" class="mw-redirect" title="Schists">schists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutchison2010a_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutchison2010a-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wei2011a_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wei2011a-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Zhen2013a_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zhen2013a-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The dismemberment and subsidence of continental crust into horsts, rotated fault blocks and half-grabens that underlie the Spratly Islands and surrounding sea bottom occurred in two distinct periods. They occurred as the result of the tectonic stretching of continental crust along underlying deeply rooted detachment faults. During the Late Cretaceous and Early <a href="/wiki/Oligocene" title="Oligocene">Oligocene</a>, the earliest period of tectonic stretching of continental crust and formation of horsts, half-grabens, and rotated fault-blocks occurred in association with the rifting and later sea-floor spreading that created the South China Sea. During the Late Oligocene-Early <a href="/wiki/Miocene" title="Miocene">Miocene</a> additional stretching and block faulting of continental crust occurred within the Spratly Islands and adjacent Dangerous Ground. During and after this period of tectonic activity, corals and other marine life colonised the crests of the horsts and other ridges that lay in shallow water. The remains of these organisms accumulated over time as biogenic carbonates that comprise the current day reefs, shoals and cays of the Spratly Islands. Starting with their formation in Late Cretaceous, fine-grained organic-rich marine sediments accumulated within the numerous submarine half-grabens that underlie sea bottom within the Dangerous Ground region.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutchison2010a_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutchison2010a-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wei2011a_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wei2011a-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Zhen2013a_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zhen2013a-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The geological surveys show localised areas within the Spratly Islands region are favourable for the accumulation of economic oil and gas reserves. They include thick sequences of <a href="/wiki/Cenozoic" title="Cenozoic">Cenozoic</a> sediments east of the Spratly Islands. Southeast and west of them, there also exist thick accumulations of sediments that possibly might contain economic oil and gas reserves, which lie closer to the Spratly Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-Owen2012a_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owen2012a-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Blanche1997a_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blanche1997a-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecology">Ecology</span></h2>
<p>In some cays in the Spratly Islands, the sand and pebble sediments form the beaches and spits around the island. Under the influence of the dominant wind direction, which changes seasonally, these sediments move around the island to change the shape and size of the island. For example, Spratly Island is larger during the northeast monsoon (about 700 × 300 meters), and smaller during the southwest monsoon (approximately 650 × 320 meters).<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some islands may contain fresh groundwater fed by rain. Groundwater levels fluctuate during the day with the rhythm of the tides.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Phosphates from bird faeces (<a href="/wiki/Guano" title="Guano">guano</a>) are mainly concentrated in the beach rocks by the way of exchange-<a href="/wiki/Endosmosis" class="mw-redirect" title="Endosmosis">endosmosis</a>. The principal minerals bearing phosphate are podolite, lewistonite and dehonite.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Coral_reefs">Coral reefs</span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Coral_reefs" class="mw-redirect" title="Coral reefs">Coral reefs</a> are the predominant structures of these islands; the Spratly group contains over 600 coral reefs in total.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In April 2015 the <i>New York Times</i> reported that China were using "scores of dredgers" to convert Fiery Cross Reef and several other reefs into military facilities.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Vegetation">Vegetation</span></h3>
<p>Little vegetation grows on these islands, which are subject to intense <a href="/wiki/Monsoons" class="mw-redirect" title="Monsoons">monsoons</a>. Larger islands are capable of supporting <a href="/wiki/Tropical_forest" title="Tropical forest">tropical forest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scrub_forest" class="mw-redirect" title="Scrub forest">scrub forest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Coastal_scrub" class="mw-redirect" title="Coastal scrub">coastal scrub</a> and grasses. It is difficult to determine which species have been introduced or cultivated by humans. <a href="/wiki/Taiping_Island" title="Taiping Island">Taiping Island</a> (Itu Aba) was reportedly covered with <a href="/wiki/Shrubs" class="mw-redirect" title="Shrubs">shrubs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Coconut" title="Coconut">coconut</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mangroves" class="mw-redirect" title="Mangroves">mangroves</a> in 1938; <a href="/wiki/Pineapple" title="Pineapple">pineapple</a> was also cultivated there when it was profitable. Other accounts mention <a href="/wiki/Papaya" title="Papaya">papaya</a>, <a href="/wiki/Banana" title="Banana">banana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Palm_tree" class="mw-redirect" title="Palm tree">palm</a>, and even <a href="/wiki/White_peach" class="mw-redirect" title="White peach">white peach</a> trees growing on one island. A few islands that have been developed as small tourist resorts had soil and trees brought in and planted where there was none.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wildlife">Wildlife</span></h3>
<p>A total of 2,927 marine species have been recorded in the Spratly Sea, including 776 <a href="/wiki/Benthic_zone" title="Benthic zone">benthic</a> species, 382 species of hard coral, 524 species of marine fish, 262 species of algae and sea grass, 35 species of seabirds, and 20 species of marine mammals and sea turtles.<sup id="cite_ref-researchgate1_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-researchgate1-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Terrestrial vegetation in the islands includes 103 species of vascular plants of magnolia branches (<a href="/wiki/Magnoliophyta" class="mw-redirect" title="Magnoliophyta">Magnoliophyta</a>) of 39 families and 79 genera.<sup id="cite_ref-researchgate1_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-researchgate1-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The islands that do have vegetation provide important habitats for many seabirds and sea turtles.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Both the <a href="/wiki/Green_turtle" class="mw-redirect" title="Green turtle">green turtle</a> (<i>Chelonia mydas</i>, <a href="/wiki/Endangered_species" title="Endangered species">endangered</a>) and the <a href="/wiki/Hawksbill_turtle" class="mw-redirect" title="Hawksbill turtle">hawksbill turtle</a> (<i>Eretmochelys imbricata</i>, <a href="/wiki/Critically_endangered" class="mw-redirect" title="Critically endangered">critically endangered</a>) formerly occurred in numbers sufficient to support commercial exploitation. These species reportedly continue to nest even on islands inhabited by military personnel (such as <a href="/wiki/Pratas" class="mw-redirect" title="Pratas">Pratas</a>) to some extent, though it is believed that their numbers have declined.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Seabirds use the islands as resting, breeding, and <a href="/wiki/Wintering" class="mw-redirect" title="Wintering">wintering</a> sites. Species found here include <a href="/wiki/Streaked_shearwater" title="Streaked shearwater">streaked shearwater</a> (<i>Calonectris leucomelas</i>), <a href="/wiki/Brown_booby" title="Brown booby">brown booby</a> (<i>Sula leucogaster</i>), <a href="/wiki/Red-footed_booby" title="Red-footed booby">red-footed booby</a> (<i>S. sula</i>), <a href="/wiki/Great_crested_tern" class="mw-redirect" title="Great crested tern">great crested tern</a> (<i>Sterna bergii</i>), and <a href="/wiki/White_tern" title="White tern">white tern</a> (<i>Gygis alba</i>). Little information is available regarding the current status of the islands' seabird populations, though it is likely that birds may divert nesting sites to smaller, less disturbed islands. Bird eggs cover the majority of <a href="/wiki/Southwest_Cay" title="Southwest Cay">Southwest Cay</a>, a small island in the eastern Danger Zone.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> A variety of <a href="/wiki/Cetacean" class="mw-redirect" title="Cetacean">cetaceans</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Dolphin" title="Dolphin">dolphins</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Orca" title="Orca">orcas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pilot_whale" title="Pilot whale">pilot whales</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Sperm_whale" title="Sperm whale">sperm whales</a> are also present around the islands.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This <a href="/wiki/Ecoregion" title="Ecoregion">ecoregion</a> is still largely a mystery. Scientists have focused their research on the marine environment, while the ecology of the terrestrial environment remains relatively unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecological_hazards">Ecological hazards</span></h3>
<p>Political instability, tourism and the increasing industrialisation of neighbouring countries has led to serious disruption of native flora and fauna, <a href="/wiki/Over-exploitation" class="mw-redirect" title="Over-exploitation">over-exploitation</a> of natural resources, and <a href="/wiki/Environmental_pollution" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental pollution">environmental pollution</a>. Disruption of nesting areas by human activity and/or by introduced animals, such as dogs, has reduced the number of turtles nesting on the islands. Sea turtles are also slaughtered for food on a significant scale. The sea turtle is a symbol of longevity in Chinese culture and at times the military personnel are given orders to protect the turtles.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Heavy commercial fishing in the region incurs other problems. Although it has been outlawed, fishing methods continue to include the use of <a href="/wiki/Bottom_trawling" title="Bottom trawling">bottom trawlers</a> fitted with chain rollers. In 1994 a routine patrol by Taiwan's marine navy confiscated more than 200&#160;kg of <a href="/wiki/Potassium_cyanide" title="Potassium cyanide">potassium cyanide</a> solution from fishermen who had been using it for <a href="/wiki/Cyanide_fishing" title="Cyanide fishing">cyanide fishing</a>. These activities have a devastating impact on local marine organisms and coral reefs.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some interest has been taken in regard to conservation of these island ecosystems. J.W. McManus, professor of marine biology and ecology at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Miami" title="University of Miami">University of Miami</a>'s <a href="/w/index.php?title=Rosenstiel_School_of_Marine,_Atmospheric,_and_Earth_Science_School&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Rosenstiel School of Marine, Atmospheric, and Earth Science School (page does not exist)">Rosenstiel School of Marine, Atmospheric, and Earth Science School</a>, has explored the possibilities of designating portions of the Spratly Islands as a <a href="/wiki/Marine_park" title="Marine park">marine park</a>. One region of the Spratly Archipelago, named Truong Sa, was proposed by Vietnam's Ministry of Science, Technology, and the Environment (MOSTE) as a future protected area. The site, with an area of 160&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (62&#160;sq&#160;mi), is currently managed by the <a href="/wiki/Kh%C3%A1nh_H%C3%B2a_Province" class="mw-redirect" title="Khánh Hòa Province">Khánh Hòa</a> Provincial People's Committee of Vietnam.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Military groups in the Spratly Islands have engaged in environmentally damaging activities such as shooting turtles and seabirds, raiding nests and fishing with explosives. The collection of rare medicinal plants, collecting of wood, and hunting for the wildlife trade are common threats to the biodiversity of the entire region, including these islands. Coral habitats are threatened by pollution, over-exploitation of fish and invertebrates, and the use of explosives and poisons as fishing techniques.<sup id="cite_ref-ECO_3-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ECO-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A 2014 <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme" title="United Nations Environment Programme">United Nations Environment Programme</a> (UNEP) report said: "Sand is rarer than one thinks".<sup id="cite_ref-UNEP201403_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNEP201403-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The average price of sand imported by Singapore was <a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">US$</a>3 per <a href="/wiki/Tonne" title="Tonne">tonne</a> from 1995 to 2001, but the price increased to US$190 per tonne from 2003 to 2005.<sup id="cite_ref-UNEP201403_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNEP201403-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Although the Philippines and China had both ratified the UNCLOS III, in the case of and <a href="/wiki/Johnson_South_Reef" title="Johnson South Reef">Johnson South Reef</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hughes_Reef" title="Hughes Reef">Hughes Reef</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mischief_Reef" title="Mischief Reef">Mischief Reef</a>, the PRC dredged sand for free in the EEZ the Philippines<sup id="cite_ref-philstarDiola2014_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-philstarDiola2014-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> had claimed from 1978<sup id="cite_ref-PD1599_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PD1599-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> arguing this is the "waters of China's Nansha Islands".
</p><p>Although the consequences of substrate mining are hidden, they are tremendous.<sup id="cite_ref-UNEP201403_33-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNEP201403-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Aggregate particles that are too fine to be used are rejected by dredging boats, releasing vast dust plumes and changing water turbidity.<sup id="cite_ref-UNEP201403_33-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNEP201403-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John McManus, a professor of marine biology and ecology at the University of Miami's Rosenstiel School of Marine, Atmospheric, and Earth Science, said, "The worst thing anyone can do to a coral reef is to bury it under tons of sand and gravel&#160;... There are global security concerns associated with the damage. It is likely broad enough to reduce fish stocks in the world's most fish-dependent region." He explained that the reason "the world has heard little about the damage inflicted by the Peoples&#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; Republic of China to the reefs is that the experts can't get to them" and noted "I have colleagues from the Philippines, Taiwan, PRC, Vietnam and Malaysia who have worked in the Spratly area. Most would not be able to get near the <a href="/wiki/Artificial_island" title="Artificial island">artificial islands</a> except possibly some from PRC, and those would not be able to release their findings."<sup id="cite_ref-breakingdefense_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-breakingdefense-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
