class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%87%91%E7%9B%BE%E6%9A%97%E6%B2%99" class="extiw" title="zh:金盾暗沙">zh</a>&#93;</span><br /></div></div></div></div>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas_Dedicada_al_Rey_Nuestro_Se%C3%B1or_por_el_Mariscal_d._Campo_D._Fernando_Valdes_Tamon_Cavall%C2%BA_del_Orden_de_Santiago_de_Govor._Y_Capn.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas_Dedicada_al_Rey_Nuestro_Se%C3%B1or_por_el_Mariscal_d._Campo_D._Fernando_Valdes_Tamon_Cavall%C2%BA_del_Orden_de_Santiago_de_Govor._Y_Capn.jpg/220px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="202" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas_Dedicada_al_Rey_Nuestro_Se%C3%B1or_por_el_Mariscal_d._Campo_D._Fernando_Valdes_Tamon_Cavall%C2%BA_del_Orden_de_Santiago_de_Govor._Y_Capn.jpg/330px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas_Dedicada_al_Rey_Nuestro_Se%C3%B1or_por_el_Mariscal_d._Campo_D._Fernando_Valdes_Tamon_Cavall%C2%BA_del_Orden_de_Santiago_de_Govor._Y_Capn.jpg/440px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4745" data-file-height="4365" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Velarde_map" title="Velarde map">Velarde map</a> shows actual Philippine control over <a href="/wiki/Scarborough_Shoal" title="Scarborough Shoal">Scarborough Shoal</a>, as well as islands off of <a href="/wiki/Palawan" title="Palawan">Palawan</a>, identified as the Spratly Islands. The map was one of the key evidences in the <i><a href="/wiki/Philippines_v._China" title="Philippines v. China">Philippines v. China</a></i> international case that debunked China's so-called nine-dash line claims.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bajos_de_Paragua,_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Bajos_de_Paragua%2C_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg/220px-Bajos_de_Paragua%2C_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Bajos_de_Paragua%2C_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg/330px-Bajos_de_Paragua%2C_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Bajos_de_Paragua%2C_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg/440px-Bajos_de_Paragua%2C_detail_in_Carta_Hydrographica_y_Chorographica_de_las_Yslas_Filipinas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="601" data-file-height="481" /></a><figcaption>The Spratlys labeled as <i>"Los Bajos de Paragua"</i> off the coast of <a href="/wiki/Palawan" title="Palawan">Palawan</a> (<i>Paragua</i>) on the 1734 <a href="/wiki/Velarde_map" title="Velarde map">Murillo Velarde map</a>. Some shoals in the Spratlys were then considered part of <a href="/wiki/Palawan" title="Palawan">Palawan</a> in the <i><a href="/wiki/Philippines_v._China" title="Philippines v. China">Philippines v. China arbitration case</a>.</i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Records show the islands as inhabited at various times in history by Chinese and Vietnamese fishermen. In 1888 the Central Borneo Company were granted a lease to work guano 'on Sprattly island and Amboyna Cay'<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a> troops from <a href="/wiki/French_Indochina" title="French Indochina">French Indochina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Navy" title="Imperial Japanese Navy">Japan</a> were in occupation.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> However, there is no record of large settlements on the islands until 1956, when Filipino adventurer <a href="/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Cloma" class="mw-redirect" title="Tomás Cloma">Tomás Cloma, Sr.</a>, decided to "claim" a part of Spratly islands as his own, naming it the "<a href="/wiki/Free_Territory_of_Freedomland" title="Free Territory of Freedomland">Free Territory of Freedomland</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Evidence of human presence in the region extends back nearly 50,000 years at <a href="/wiki/Tabon_Caves" title="Tabon Caves">Tabon Caves</a> on Palawan.  Therefore, it is difficult to say when humans first came upon this island group. Within historical times, several groups may have passed through or occupied the islands. Between 600&#160;BC to 3&#160;BC there was an east to west migration by members of the seafaring <a href="/wiki/Sa_Hu%E1%BB%B3nh_culture" title="Sa Huỳnh culture">Sa Huỳnh culture</a>. This may have led them through the Spratly Islands on their way to Vietnam. These migrants were the forebears of the <a href="/wiki/Cham_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Cham people">Cham people</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_languages" title="Austronesian languages">Austronesian-speaking</a> people that founded the Old <a href="/wiki/Champa" title="Champa">Champa</a> empire that ruled what was known for centuries as the Champa Sea.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
<sup id="cite_ref-Cham_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cham-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_records_and_cartography">Early records and cartography</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg/220px-MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="308" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg/330px-MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg/440px-MAO_KUN_MAP-19.jpg 2x" data-file-width="450" data-file-height="631" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Mao_Kun_map" title="Mao Kun map">Mao Kun map</a>, Spratly Islands is suggested to be the islands at the bottom right (<span lang="zh">石星石塘</span>; <i><span lang="zh-Latn-pinyin">shíxīng shítáng</span></i>).<sup id="cite_ref-security_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-security-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Others however believe the islands may refer to the <a href="/wiki/Paracel_Islands" title="Paracel Islands">Paracel Islands</a> or <a href="/wiki/Macclesfield_Bank" title="Macclesfield Bank">Macclesfield Bank</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-mills_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mills-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Song_Dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Song Dynasty">Song Dynasty</a> work <i><a href="/wiki/Zhu_fan_zhi" class="mw-redirect" title="Zhu fan zhi">Zhu fan zhi</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Zhao_Rugua" class="mw-redirect" title="Zhao Rugua">Zhao Rugua</a>, the name "Thousand <a href="/wiki/Li_(unit)" title="Li (unit)">Li</a> Stretch of Sands" (<i>Qianli Changsha</i>, <span title="Chinese-language text"><span lang="zh-Hant">千里長沙</span></span>) and the "Ten-Thousand <a href="/wiki/Li_(unit)" title="Li (unit)">Li</a> of Stone Pools/Beds" (<i>Wanli Shitang</i> <span title="Chinese-language text"><span lang="zh-Hant">萬里石塘</span></span>, or <i>Wanli Shichuang</i> <span title="Chinese-language text"><span lang="zh"><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E8%90%AC%E9%87%8C" class="extiw" title="wikt:萬里">萬里</a><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E7%9F%B3" class="extiw" title="wikt:石">石</a><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E5%BA%8A" class="extiw" title="wikt:床">床</a></span></span>) were given, interpreted by some to refer to <a href="/wiki/Paracel_Islands" title="Paracel Islands">Paracel</a> and Spratly respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>  <i>Wanli Shitang</i> is also recorded in the <i><a href="/wiki/History_of_Yuan" title="History of Yuan">History of Yuan</a></i> to have been explored by the Chinese during the Mongol-led <a href="/wiki/Yuan_dynasty" title="Yuan dynasty">Yuan dynasty</a> and may have been considered by them to have been within their national boundaries.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> However, the Yuan also ruled over <a href="/wiki/Korea" title="Korea">Korea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mongolia" title="Mongolia">Outer Mongolia</a>, and parts of modern <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>. They are also referenced, sometimes with different names, in the Ming dynasty.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-miscrec_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miscrec-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> For example, in the <a href="/wiki/Mao_Kun_map" title="Mao Kun map">Mao Kun map</a> dating from <a href="/wiki/Zheng_He" title="Zheng He">Zheng He</a>'s voyage of the early 15th century, <i>Shixing Shitang</i> (<span title="Chinese-language text"><span lang="zh">石星石塘</span></span>) is taken by some to mean Spratly,<sup id="cite_ref-security_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-security-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> however different authors interpret the identities of these islands differently.<sup id="cite_ref-mills_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mills-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Another Ming text, <i>Haiyu</i> (海語, On the Sea), uses <i>Wanli Changsha</i> (<span title="Chinese-language text"><span lang="zh">萬里長沙</span></span>) for Spratly and noted that it is located southeast of <i>Wanli Shitang</i> (Paracels).<sup id="cite_ref-security_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-security-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> When the Ming Dynasty collapsed, the <a href="/wiki/Qing_dynasty" title="Qing dynasty">Qing dynasty</a> continued to include the territory in maps compiled in 1724,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> 1755,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> 1767,<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> 1810,<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> and 1817,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> but did not officially claim jurisdiction over these islands. 
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_(_Singapore,_Borneo,_Sumatra,_Java,_Philippines)_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_%28_Singapore%2C_Borneo%2C_Sumatra%2C_Java%2C_Philippines%29_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg/220px-1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_%28_Singapore%2C_Borneo%2C_Sumatra%2C_Java%2C_Philippines%29_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="194" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_%28_Singapore%2C_Borneo%2C_Sumatra%2C_Java%2C_Philippines%29_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg/330px-1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_%28_Singapore%2C_Borneo%2C_Sumatra%2C_Java%2C_Philippines%29_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_%28_Singapore%2C_Borneo%2C_Sumatra%2C_Java%2C_Philippines%29_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg/440px-1801_Cary_Map_of_the_East_Indies_and_Southeast_Asia_%28_Singapore%2C_Borneo%2C_Sumatra%2C_Java%2C_Philippines%29_-_Geographicus_-_EastIndies-cary-1801.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5000" data-file-height="4406" /></a><figcaption>An 1801 Cary Map of the East Indies and Southeast Asia, with isles similar to those in the Mao Kun map. The isles are not the Paracels or Spratly Islands, but are instead identified as "Pulo Taya" and "Amphitrite".</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg/220px-DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="312" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg/330px-DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg/440px-DaiNamNhatThongToanDo_1834-1838.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1146" data-file-height="1623" /></a><figcaption>An 1838 Unified Dai Nam map marking <a href="/wiki/Tr%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Dng_Sa" class="mw-redirect" title="Trường Sa">Trường Sa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ho%C3%A0ng_Sa" class="mw-redirect" title="Hoàng Sa">Hoàng Sa</a>, which are considered as Spratly and <a href="/wiki/Paracel_Islands" title="Paracel Islands">Paracel Islands</a> by some Vietnamese scholars; yet they share different latitude, location, shape and distance. The Paracel claim may have been correctly identified, however, the "Spratly" claim on the map may instead be islands nearer to Vietnamese shores<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Avoid_weasel_words" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:Avoid weasel words"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too vague attribution or weasel words. (December 2020)">which?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>, instead of the actual Spratly Islands.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A Vietnamese map from 1834 also combines the Spratly and Paracel Islands into one region known as "Vạn Lý Trường Sa", a feature commonly incorporated into maps of the era (<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1094882035">.mw-parser-output .script-Cprt{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Segoe UI Historic","Noto Sans Cypriot",Code2001}.mw-parser-output .script-Hano{font-size:125%;font-family:"Noto Sans Hanunoo",FreeSerif,Quivira}.mw-parser-output .script-Latf,.mw-parser-output .script-de-Latf{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Breitkopf Fraktur",UnifrakturCook,UniFrakturMaguntia,MarsFraktur,"MarsFraktur OT",KochFraktur,"KochFraktur OT",OffenbacherSchwabOT,"LOB.AlteSchwabacher","LOV.AlteSchwabacher","LOB.AtlantisFraktur","LOV.AtlantisFraktur","LOB.BreitkopfFraktur","LOV.BreitkopfFraktur","LOB.FetteFraktur","LOV.FetteFraktur","LOB.Fraktur3","LOV.Fraktur3","LOB.RochFraktur","LOV.RochFraktur","LOB.PostFraktur","LOV.PostFraktur","LOB.RuelhscheFraktur","LOV.RuelhscheFraktur","LOB.RungholtFraktur","LOV.RungholtFraktur","LOB.TheuerbankFraktur","LOV.TheuerbankFraktur","LOB.VinetaFraktur","LOV.VinetaFraktur","LOB.WalbaumFraktur","LOV.WalbaumFraktur","LOB.WeberMainzerFraktur","LOV.WeberMainzerFraktur","LOB.WieynckFraktur","LOV.WieynckFraktur","LOB.ZentenarFraktur","LOV.ZentenarFraktur"}.mw-parser-output .script-en-Latf{font-size:1.25em;font-family:Cankama,"Old English Text MT","Textura Libera","Textura Libera Tenuis",London}.mw-parser-output .script-it-Latf{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Rotunda Pommerania",Rotunda,"Typographer Rotunda"}.mw-parser-output .script-Lina{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Noto Sans Linear A"}.mw-parser-output .script-Linb{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Noto Sans Linear B"}.mw-parser-output .script-Ugar{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Segoe UI Historic","Noto Sans Ugaritic",Aegean}.mw-parser-output .script-Xpeo{font-size:1.25em;font-family:"Segoe UI Historic","Noto Sans Old Persian",Artaxerxes,Xerxes,Aegean}</style><span class="Unicode">萬里長沙</span>) ‒ that is, the same as the aforementioned Chinese island name <i>Wanli Changsha</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Hanoi" title="Hanoi">Hanoi</a>, Vietnamese maps record Bãi Cát Vàng (<i>Golden Sandbanks</i>, referring to both the Spratly and Paracel Islands), which lay near the coast of the central Vietnam, as early as 1838.<sup id="cite_ref-KING_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KING-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> In <i>Phủ Biên Tạp Lục</i> (<i>The Frontier Chronicles</i>) by scholar <a href="/wiki/L%C3%AA_Qu%C3%BD_%C4%90%C3%B4n" title="Lê Quý Đôn">Lê Quý Đôn</a>, both <a href="/wiki/Ho%C3%A0ng_Sa" class="mw-redirect" title="Hoàng Sa">Hoàng Sa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tr%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Dng_Sa" class="mw-redirect" title="Trường Sa">Trường Sa</a> were defined as belonging to the Quảng Ngãi District. He described it as where sea products and shipwrecked cargoes were available to be collected. Vietnamese text written in the 17th century referenced government-sponsored economic activities during the <a href="/wiki/L%C3%AA_dynasty" title="Lê dynasty">Lê dynasty</a>, 200&#160;years earlier. The Vietnamese government conducted several geographical surveys of the islands in the 18th century.<sup id="cite_ref-KING_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KING-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:China_Sea_-_Southern_Portion_-_Eastern_Sheet.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/China_Sea_-_Southern_Portion_-_Eastern_Sheet.png/220px-China_Sea_-_Southern_Portion_-_Eastern_Sheet.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="322" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/China_Sea_-_Southern_Portion_-_Eastern_Sheet.png/330px-China_Sea_-_Southern_Portion_-_Eastern_Sheet.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7c/China_Sea_-_Southern_Portion_-_Eastern_Sheet.png 2x" data-file-width="436" data-file-height="639" /></a><figcaption>A striking large black and white British chart of the sea in northern Borneo, first issued in 1881 and corrected to 1935.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite the fact that China and Vietnam both made a claim to these territories simultaneously, at the time, neither side was aware that its neighbour had already charted and made claims to the same stretch of islands.<sup id="cite_ref-KING_60-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KING-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>An early European map, <i>A correct chart of the China Seas</i> of 1758 by William Herbert, left the Spratly Islands region (known then as the Dangerous Ground) as largely blank, indicating that region has yet to be properly surveyed, although some islands and shoals at its western edge were marked (one appears at the same place as <a href="/wiki/Thitu_Island" title="Thitu Island">Thitu Island</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-hancox_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hancox-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> A number of maps of the South China Sea were later produced, but the first map that gives a reasonably accurate delineation of the Spratly Islands region (titled <i>[South] China Sea, Sheet 1</i>) was only published in 1821 by the hydrographer of the East India Company James Horsburgh after a survey by Captain Daniel Ross. A later 1859 edition of the map named the Spratly Island as Storm Island.<sup id="cite_ref-hancox_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hancox-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The islands were sporadically visited throughout the 19th and early 20th centuries by mariners from different European powers (including <a href="/wiki/Richard_Spratly" title="Richard Spratly">Richard Spratly</a>, after whom the island group derives its most recognisable English name, who visited the group in the 1840s in his <a href="/wiki/Whaler" title="Whaler">whaler</a> <a href="/wiki/Cyrus_(1800_whaler)" title="Cyrus (1800 whaler)"><i>Cyrus</i></a>).<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> However, these nations showed little interest in the islands.  In 1883, German boats surveyed the Spratly and the Paracel Islands but eventually withdrew the survey, after receiving protests from the <a href="/wiki/Guangdong" title="Guangdong">Guangdong</a> government representing the <a href="/wiki/Qing_dynasty" title="Qing dynasty">Qing dynasty</a>.  China sent naval forces on inspection tours in 1902 and 1907 and placed flags and markers on the islands.<sup id="cite_ref-Severino2011_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Severino2011-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 1950s, a group of individuals claimed sovereignty over the islands in the name of Morton F. Meads, supposedly an American descendant of a British naval captain who gave his name to Meads Island (Itu Aba) in the 1870s. In an affidavit made in 1971, the group claimed to represent the Kingdom of Humanity/<a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Morac-Songhrati-Meads" title="Republic of Morac-Songhrati-Meads">Republic of Morac-Songhrati-Meads</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> which they asserted was in turn the successor entity for a supposed Kingdom of Humanity established between the two world wars on Meads Island, allegedly by the son of the British captain. This claim to this would-be <a href="/wiki/Micronation" title="Micronation">micronation</a> fell dormant after 1972, when several members of the group drowned in a typhoon.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-fowler_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fowler-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-latimes_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-latimes-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_conflicts_and_diplomatic_dialogues">Military conflicts and diplomatic dialogues</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Spratly_Islands_dispute" title="Spratly Islands dispute">Spratly Islands dispute</a></div>
<p>The following are political divisions for the Spratly Islands claimed by various area nations (in alphabetical order):
</p>
<ul><li>Brunei: Part of Brunei's Exclusive Economic Zone<sup id="cite_ref-Brunei_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brunei-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>China: Part of <a href="/wiki/Sansha" title="Sansha">Sansha</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hainan" title="Hainan">Hainan</a><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Malaysia: Part of <a href="/wiki/Sabah" title="Sabah">Sabah</a> state</li>
<li>Philippines: Part of <a href="/wiki/Kalayaan,_Palawan" title="Kalayaan, Palawan">Kalayaan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Palawan" title="Palawan">Palawan</a> province</li>
<li>Taiwan: Part of <a href="/wiki/Kaohsiung" title="Kaohsiung">Kaohsiung</a> municipality</li>
<li>Vietnam: Part of <a href="/wiki/Tr%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Dng_Sa_District" class="mw-redirect" title="Trường Sa District">Trường Sa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kh%C3%A1nh_H%C3%B2a_Province" class="mw-redirect" title="Khánh Hòa Province">Khánh Hòa Province</a></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflicts_in_the_19th_century">Conflicts in the 19th century</span></h4>
<p>In the 19th century, Europeans found that Chinese fishermen from <a href="/wiki/Hainan" title="Hainan">Hainan</a> annually sojourned on the Spratly islands for part of the year, while in 1877 it was the British who launched the first modern legal claims to the Spratly Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-Kivimaki2002_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kivimaki2002-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Taylor_&amp;_Francis_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Taylor_&amp;_Francis-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chinese scholars and officials often try to claim that the 1887 Sino-French Tonkin Boundary convention signed between France and China after the <a href="/wiki/Sino-French_War" title="Sino-French War">Sino-French War</a> recognised the sovereignty of China over of the Spratly and Paracel islands.<sup id="cite_ref-Wortzel_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wortzel-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> However, it is very clear that the Convention never mention a single word about Spratly and Paracel islands, nor South China Sea.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Professor Zou Keyuan, after examining carefully the text of the Convention, concluded that there was no mention of the Gulf of Tonkin and only part of the Gulf close to the land was shown on the attached map. Thus the representatives from both parties had no authorisation and/or intention to delimit the maritime boundary in the Gulf of Tonkin. A similar argument can be made with respect to a determination of sovereignty of the South China Sea islands. Chinese and French negotiators clearly did not have the authorisation or the intent to make such a determination in 1887.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> France had already expressed the similar view in a diplomatic note dated back to October 10, 1937:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p><i>The provisions of the 1887 Treaty... had no other object but to fix the maritime frontier between China and Tonkin in the region of <a href="/wiki/M%C3%B3ng_C%C3%A1i" title="Móng Cái">Monkai</a>, attaching to China some territories and islands situated east of the mouth of the River Monkai and which were formerly under Annam. To simplify matters, the 105°43' Paris meridian was chosen as the demarcation line. However, the text of the agreement clearly shows that the clause at issue specifically refers to the Monkai region. To seek to apply it to the Paracels, which are situated almost 300 nautical miles south‐east, would amount to saying that everything east of the 105°43' meridian belongs to China. China could therefore lay claim to most of the coastal islands of Indochina, <a href="/wiki/Poulo_Condor" class="mw-redirect" title="Poulo Condor">Poulo Condor</a> among them! The absurd consequences of such an argument clearly show that only local scope and significance should be given to the clause in the 1887 Convention.</i><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The Chinese Ministry of Foreign Affairs also try to claim that a 1883 incident involving a German ship conducting surveys in the South China Sea without China’s consent was protested with Berlin and the Germans terminated the survey.<sup id="cite_ref-Severino2011_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Severino2011-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>  Western scholars have determined, however, that this incident is not based on verifiable references and is inconsistent with other Chinese inaction during the same time period given that, in 1885, the German Admiralty published a two‐sheet chart entitled <i>Die Paracel‐Inseln</i> (The Paracel Islands). The chart documented the work of a German expedition to the Paracels between 1881 and 1884.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflicts_in_the_20th_century_until_1945">Conflicts in the 20th century until 1945</span></h4>
<p>China sent naval forces on inspection tours in 1902 and 1907 and placed flags and markers on the islands. The Qing dynasty's successor state, the Republic of China, claimed the Spratly and Paracel islands under the jurisdiction of Hainan.<sup id="cite_ref-Severino2011_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Severino2011-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1933, France asserted its claims to the Spratly and Paracel Islands<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> on behalf of its then-colony <a href="/wiki/French_Indochina" title="French Indochina">French Indochina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-encarta_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encarta-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> It occupied a number of the Spratly Islands, including <a href="/wiki/Taiping_Island" title="Taiping Island">Taiping Island</a>, built weather stations on two of the islands, and administered them as part of French Indochina. This occupation was protested by the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%9349)" class="mw-redirect" title="Republic of China (1912–49)">Republic of China</a> (ROC) government because France admitted finding Chinese fishermen there when French warships visited nine of the islands.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> In 1935, the ROC government also announced a sovereignty claim on the Spratly Islands. <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Japan</a> occupied some of the islands in 1939 during World War II, and it used the islands as a <a href="/wiki/Submarine" title="Submarine">submarine</a> base for the occupation of Southeast Asia. During the Japanese occupation, these islands were called <i>Shinnan Shoto</i> (<span title="Japanese-language text"><span lang="ja">新南諸島</span></span>), literally the New Southern Islands, and together with the Paracel Islands (<span title="Chinese-language text"><span lang="zh-Hans">西沙群岛</span></span>), they were put under the governance of the Japanese colonial authority in <a href="/wiki/Taiwan_under_Japanese_rule" title="Taiwan under Japanese rule">Taiwan</a>.
</p><p>Japan occupied the Paracels and the Spratly Islands from February 1939 to August 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-king1979p43_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-king1979p43-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Japan annexed the Spratly Islands via Taiwan's jurisdiction and the Paracels via Hainan's jurisdiction.<sup id="cite_ref-Kivimaki2002_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kivimaki2002-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Parts of the Paracels and Spratly Islands were again controlled by Republic of China after the 1945 surrender of Japan,<sup id="cite_ref-ed._Morley,_Nishihara_1997_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ed._Morley,_Nishihara_1997-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> since the Allied powers assigned the Republic of China to receive Japanese surrenders in that area,<sup id="cite_ref-Severino2011_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Severino2011-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> however no successor was named to the islands.<sup id="cite_ref-ed._Morley,_Nishihara_1997_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ed._Morley,_Nishihara_1997-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflicts_in_the_20th_century_after_World_War_II">Conflicts in the 20th century after World War II</span></h4>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Map of the South East China" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png/200px-1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png/300px-1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png/401px-1947_Nanhai_Zhudao.png 2x" data-file-width="2654" data-file-height="3840" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%931949)" title="Republic of China (1912–1949)">China</a>'s (now <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">ROC</a> and <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">PRC</a>) <a href="/wiki/Nine-dash_line" title="Nine-dash line">nine-dash line claim</a> over the <a href="/wiki/South_China_Sea" title="South China Sea">South China Sea</a>, 1947</figcaption></figure>
<p>In November 1946, the ROC sent naval ships to take control of the islands after the <a href="/wiki/Surrender_of_Japan" title="Surrender of Japan">surrender of Japan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-king1979p43_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-king1979p43-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> It had chosen the largest and perhaps the only inhabitable island, <a href="/wiki/Taiping_Island" title="Taiping Island">Taiping Island</a>, as its base, and it renamed the island under the name of the naval vessel as Taiping. Also following the defeat of Japan at the end of World War II, the ROC re-claimed the entirety of the Spratly Islands (including Taiping Island) after accepting the Japanese surrender of the islands based on the <a href="/wiki/1943_Cairo_Declaration" title="1943 Cairo Declaration">Cairo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Potsdam_Declaration" title="Potsdam Declaration">Potsdam Declarations</a>. The Republic of China then garrisoned Itu Aba (Taiping) island in 1946 and posted Chinese flags.<sup id="cite_ref-Kivimaki2002_72-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kivimaki2002-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The aim of the Republic of China was to block the French claims.<sup id="cite_ref-Severino2011_64-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Severino2011-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Editors_of_Encyclopædia_Britannica_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Editors_of_Encyclopædia_Britannica-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> The Republic of China drew up the map showing the U-shaped claim on the entire South China Sea, showing the Spratly and Paracels in Chinese territory, in 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-Severino2011_64-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Severino2011-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Japan had renounced all claims to the islands in the 1951 <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco_Peace_Treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="San Francisco Peace Treaty">San Francisco Peace Treaty</a> together with the Paracels, Pratas and other islands captured from the Chinese, and upon these declarations, the government of the Republic of China reasserted its claim to the islands. The Chinese <a href="/wiki/Kuomintang" title="Kuomintang">Kuomintang</a> force withdrew from most of the Spratly and Paracel Islands after they retreated to Taiwan from the opposing <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party" title="Chinese Communist Party">Chinese Communist Party</a> due to their losses in the <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War" title="Chinese Civil War">Chinese Civil War</a> and the founding of the People's Republic of China (PRC) in 1949.<sup id="cite_ref-encarta_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encarta-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Taiwan quietly withdrew troops from Taiping Island in 1950, but then reinstated them in 1956 in response to <a href="/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Cloma" class="mw-redirect" title="Tomás Cloma">Tomás Cloma</a>'s sudden claim to the island as part of <a href="/wiki/Free_Territory_of_Freedomland" title="Free Territory of Freedomland">Freedomland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> As of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Spratly_Islands&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, Taiping Island is administered by Taiwan.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After pulling out its garrison in 1950 when the Republic of China evacuated to Taiwan, when the Filipino Tomas Cloma uprooted an ROC flag on Itu Aba laid claim to the Spratly Islands and, Taiwan again regarrisoned Itu Aba in 1956.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> In 1946, the Americans allegedly reminded the Philippines at its independence that the Spratly Islands were not Philippine territory, both to not anger <a href="/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek" title="Chiang Kai-shek">Chiang Kai-shek</a> in China and because the Spratly Islands were not part of the Philippines per the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1898)" title="Treaty of Paris (1898)">1898 treaty Spain signed with the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kivimaki2002_72-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kivimaki2002-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> However, no document was found to that effect. The Philippines then claimed the Spratly Islands in 1971 under <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos" title="Ferdinand Marcos">President Marcos</a>, after Taiwanese troops attacked and shot at a Philippine fishing boat on Itu Aba.<sup id="cite_ref-Pak2000_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pak2000-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taiwan's garrison from 1946 to 1950 and 1956-now on Itu Aba represents an "effective occupation" of the Spratly Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-Pak2000_87-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pak2000-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Lin2008_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lin2008-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> China established a coastal defence system against Japanese pirates or smugglers.<sup id="cite_ref-Pak2000p81_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pak2000p81-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG/170px-Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG/255px-Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG/340px-Bia_VNCH_Truong_Sa_-_Republic_of_Vietnam_Spratly_Islands_Territorial_Marker.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1704" data-file-height="2272" /></a><figcaption>Territorial monument of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Vietnam" class="mw-redirect" title="Republic of Vietnam">Republic of Vietnam</a> (South Vietnam) on <a href="/wiki/Southwest_Cay" title="Southwest Cay">Southwest Cay</a>, Spratly Islands, defining the cay as part of Vietnamese territory (<a href="/wiki/Ph%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Bc_Tuy_Province" class="mw-redirect" title="Phước Tuy Province">Phước Tuy Province</a>). Used from 22 August 1956 until 1975, when replaced by another one from the <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Vietnam" class="mw-redirect" title="Socialist Republic of Vietnam">Socialist Republic of Vietnam</a> (successor state after the <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Saigon" title="Fall of Saigon">Fall of Saigon</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1958, China issued a declaration defining its territorial waters that encompassed the Spratly Islands. <a href="/wiki/North_Vietnam" title="North Vietnam">North Vietnam</a>'s prime minister, <a href="/wiki/Ph%E1%BA%A1m_V%C4%83n_%C4%90%E1%BB%93ng" title="Phạm Văn Đồng">Phạm Văn Đồng</a>, sent a formal note to <a href="/wiki/Zhou_Enlai" title="Zhou Enlai">Zhou Enlai</a>, stating that the Government of the Democratic Republic of Vietnam (DRV) respected the Chinese decision regarding the 12&#160;nmi (22&#160;km; 14&#160;mi) limit of territorial waters.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> While accepting the 12-nmi principal with respect to territorial waters, the letter did not actually address the issue of defining actual territorial boundaries.
North Vietnam recognised China's claims on the Paracels and Spratly Islands during the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a> as it was being supported by China. Only after winning the war and conquering <a href="/wiki/South_Vietnam" title="South Vietnam">South Vietnam</a> did <a href="/wiki/North_Vietnam" title="North Vietnam">North Vietnam</a> retract its recognition and admitted it recognised them as part of China to receive aid from China in fighting the Americans.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1987, China installed a small military structure on <a href="/wiki/Fiery_Cross_Reef" title="Fiery Cross Reef">Fiery Cross Reef</a> under the pretext of building an oceanic observation station and installing a <a href="/wiki/Tide_gauge" title="Tide gauge">tide gauge</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Global_Sea_Level_Observing_System" title="Global Sea Level Observing System">Global Sea Level Observing System</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag needs to be fact-checked with the cited source(s). (October 2021)">verification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> After a <a href="/wiki/Johnson_South_Reef_Skirmish" title="Johnson South Reef Skirmish">deadly skirmish</a> with the Vietnamese Navy, China installed some military structures on more reefs in the vicinity of the Philippines and Vietnamese occupied islands and this led to escalating tensions between these countries and China over the status and ownership of reefs.
</p><p>In 1988, the Vietnamese and Chinese navies engaged in a <a href="/wiki/Johnson_South_Reef_Skirmish" title="Johnson South Reef Skirmish">skirmish in the area of Johnson South Reef</a> (also called Gạc Ma Reef in Vietnam and Yongshu Reef in China).<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Under President <a href="/wiki/Lee_Teng-hui" title="Lee Teng-hui">Lee Teng-hui</a>, Taiwan stated that "legally, historically, geographically, or in reality", all of the South China Sea and Spratly islands were Taiwan's territory and under Taiwanese sovereignty, and denounced actions undertaken there by Malaysia and the Philippines, in a statement on 13 July 1999 released by the foreign ministry of Taiwan.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Taiwan and China's claims "mirror" each other; during international talks involving the Spratly islands, China and Taiwan have cooperated with each other since both have the same claims.<sup id="cite_ref-Pak2000_87-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pak2000-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was unclear whether France continued its claim to the islands after World War II, since none of the islands, other than Taiping Island, was habitable. The <a href="/wiki/South_Vietnam" title="South Vietnam">South Vietnamese</a> government took over the <a href="/wiki/Tr%C6%B0%E1%BB%9Dng_Sa" class="mw-redirect" title="Trường Sa">Trường Sa</a> administration after the defeat of the French at the end of the <a href="/wiki/First_Indochina_War" title="First Indochina War">First Indochina War</a>. "The French bestowed its titles, rights, and claims over the two island chains to the Republic of Vietnam (RoV) in accordance with the <a href="/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference" title="1954 Geneva Conference">Geneva Accords</a>", said Nguyen Hong Thao, Associate Professor at Faculty of Law, Vietnam National University.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1999, a Philippine navy ship (Number 57 – <a href="/wiki/BRP_Sierra_Madre_(LT-57)" class="mw-redirect" title="BRP Sierra Madre (LT-57)">BRP <i>Sierra Madre</i></a>) was purposely run aground near <a href="/wiki/Second_Thomas_Shoal" title="Second Thomas Shoal">Second Thomas Shoal</a> to enable establishment of an outpost. As of 2014<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Spratly_Islands&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> it had not been removed, and Filipino marines have been stationed aboard since the grounding.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflicts_in_the_21st_century">Conflicts in the 21st century</span></h4>
<p>Taiwan and mainland China are largely strategically aligned on the Spratly islands issue, since they both claim exactly the same area, so Taiwan's control of Itu Aba (Taiping) island is viewed as an extension of China's claim.<sup id="cite_ref-Wortzel_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wortzel-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Taiwan and China both claim the entire island chain, while all the other claimants only claim portions of them. China has proposed co-operation with Taiwan against all the other countries claiming the islands. Taiwanese lawmakers have demanded that Taiwan fortify Itu Aba (Taiping) island with weapons to defend against the Vietnamese, and both China and Taiwanese NGOs have pressured Taiwan to expand Taiwan's military capabilities on the island, which played a role in Taiwan expanding the island's runway in 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-KastnerAug2012_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KastnerAug2012-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> China has urged Taiwan to co-operate and offered Taiwan a share in oil and gas resources while shutting out all the other rival claimants. Taiwanese lawmakers have complained about repeated Vietnamese aggression and trespassing on Taiwan's Itu Aba (Taiping), and Taiwan has started viewing Vietnam as an enemy over the Spratly Islands, not China.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Taiwan's state run oil company <a href="/wiki/CPC_Corporation" title="CPC Corporation">CPC Corporation</a>'s board director Chiu Yi has called Vietnam as the "greatest threat" to Taiwan.<sup id="cite_ref-KastnerAug2012_100-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KastnerAug2012-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Taiwan's airstrip on Taiping has irritated Vietnam.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> China views Taiwan's expansion of its military and airstrip on Taiping as benefiting China's position against the other rival claimants from southeast Asian countries.<sup id="cite_ref-Lin2008_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lin2008-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> China's claims to the Spratly Islands benefit from legal weight because of Taiwan's presence on Itu Aba, while America on the other hand has regularly ignored Taiwan's claims in the South China Sea and does not include Taiwan in any talks on dispute resolution for the area.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taiwan performed live fire military exercises on Taiping island in September 2012; reports said that Vietnam was explicitly named by the Taiwanese military as the "imaginary enemy" in the drill. Vietnam protested against the exercises as violation of its territory and "voiced anger", demanding that Taiwan stop the drill. Among the inspectors of the live fire drill were Taiwanese national legislators, adding to the tensions.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 2011, Chinese patrol boats attacked two Vietnamese oil exploration ships near the Spratly Islands.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Also in May 2011, Chinese naval vessels opened fire on Vietnamese fishing vessels operating off East London Reef (Da Dong). The three Chinese military vessels were numbered 989, 27 and 28, and they showed up with a small group of Chinese fishing vessels. Another Vietnamese fishing vessel was fired on near <a href="/wiki/Fiery_Cross_Reef" title="Fiery Cross Reef">Fiery Cross Reef</a> (Chu Thap). The Chief Commander of Border Guards in Phú Yên Province, Vietnam, reported that a total of four Vietnamese vessels were fired upon by Chinese naval vessels.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (July 2016)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> These incidents involving Chinese forces sparked mass protests in Vietnam, especially in <a href="/wiki/Hanoi" title="Hanoi">Hanoi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh_City" title="Ho Chi Minh City">Ho Chi Minh City</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> and in various Vietnamese communities in the West (namely in the US state of California and in Paris) over attacks on Vietnamese citizens and the intrusion into what Vietnam claimed was part of its territory.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2011, the Philippines began officially referring to parts of the South China Sea as the "<a href="/wiki/West_Philippine_Sea" title="West Philippine Sea">West Philippine Sea</a>" and the <a href="/wiki/Reed_Bank" class="mw-redirect" title="Reed Bank">Reed Bank</a> as "Recto Bank".<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 2012, the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Vietnam" title="National Assembly of Vietnam">National Assembly of Vietnam</a> passed a law demarcating Vietnamese sea borders to include the Spratly and Paracel Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2010, it was reported that the former Malaysian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Mahathir_Mohamad" title="Mahathir Mohamad">Mahathir Mohamad</a> believed Malaysia could profit from China's economic growth through co-operation with China,<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> and said that China "was not a threat to anyone and was not worried about aggression from China", as well accusing the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> of provoking China and trying to turn China's neighbours against China.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Malaysian authorities displayed no concern over China conducting a military exercise at James Shoal in March 2013,<sup id="cite_ref-philstarDiola2014_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-philstarDiola2014-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> with its <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_(Malaysia)" title="Ministry of Defence (Malaysia)">Defence Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Hishammuddin_Hussein" title="Hishammuddin Hussein">Hishammuddin Hussein</a> suggested they might work with China and saying that Malaysia had no problem with China patrolling the South China Sea, and telling ASEAN, America, and Japan that "Just because you have enemies, doesn't mean your enemies are my enemies".<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> However, until present Malaysia still maintained a balance relations with the countries involved in this dispute.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> But since China has started encroaching its territorial waters,<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Malaysia has become active in condemning China.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The editorial of the Taiwanese news website <i>Want China Times</i> accused America of being behind the May 2014 flareup in the South China Sea, saying that Vietnam rammed a Chinese vessel on 2 May over an oil rig drilling platform and the Philippines detained 11 Chinese fishermens occurred because of Obama's visit to the region and that they were incited by America "behind the scenes". <i>Want China Times</i> claimed America ordered Vietnam on 7 May to complain about the drilling platform, and noted that a joint military exercise was happening at this time between the Philippines and America, and also noted that the American <i>New York Times</i> newspaper supported Vietnam.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a series of news stories on 16 April 2015, it was revealed, through photos taken by <a href="/wiki/Airbus" title="Airbus">Airbus</a>, that China had been building an airstrip on Fiery Cross Reef, one of the southern islands. The 10,000-foot-long (3,048&#160;m) runway covers a significant portion of the island, and is viewed as a possible strategic threat to other countries with claims to the islands, such as <a href="/wiki/Vietnam" title="Vietnam">Vietnam</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>.
</p><p>Champa historically had a large presence in the South China Sea. The Vietnamese broke Champa's power in <a href="/wiki/Cham%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War_(1471)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cham–Vietnamese War (1471)">an invasion of Champa in 1471</a>, and then finally conquered the last remnants of the Cham people in an invasion in 1832. A Cham named Katip Suma who received Islamic education in Kelantan declared a <a href="/wiki/Jihad" title="Jihad">Jihad</a> against the Vietnamese, and fighting continued until the Vietnamese crushed the remnants of the resistance in 1835. The Cham organisation Front de Libération du Champa was part of the <a href="/wiki/United_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Oppressed_Races" title="United Front for the Liberation of Oppressed Races">United Front for the Liberation of Oppressed Races</a>, which waged war against the Vietnamese for independence in the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a> along with the <a href="/wiki/Degar" class="mw-redirect" title="Degar">Montagnard</a> and <a href="/wiki/Khmer_Krom" title="Khmer Krom">Khmer Krom</a> minorities. The last remaining FULRO insurgents surrendered to the United Nations in 1992.
</p><p>The Vietnamese government fears that evidence of <a href="/wiki/Champa" title="Champa">Champa</a>'s influence over the disputed area in the South China Sea would bring attention to human rights violations and killings of ethnic minorities in Vietnam such as in the 2001 and 2004 uprisings, and lead to the issue of Cham autonomy being brought into the dispute, since the Vietnamese conquered the Hindu and Muslim <a href="/wiki/Cham_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Cham people">Cham people</a> in a war in 1832.<sup id="cite_ref-Bray_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bray-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Japanese scholar Taoka Shunji said in a journal article that the assumption amongst many Japanese people that the territory of the Philippines was being invaded by China, was incorrect. He pointed out that the Spratly islands were not part of the Philippines, when the US acquired the Philippines from Spain in the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1898)" title="Treaty of Paris (1898)">Treaty of Paris in 1898</a>, and when the Japanese-ruled Taiwan itself had annexed the Spratly islands in 1938, the US-ruled Philippines did not challenge the move and never asserted that it was their territory. He also pointed out that other countries did not need to do full land reclamation since they already control islands, and that the reason China engaged in extensive land reclamation is because they needed it to build airfields since China only has control over reefs.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The "Moro" as "people", is used to describe both the Filipino Muslims and their homeland. Ancestors of Moro people were the owners of Spratly Islands prior to the arrival of the Spanish colonials in the 16th century, according to the Sultan of Sulu in the southern Philippines reported in a local paper. "China has no right over the Spratly Islands in what it calls the South China Sea because that is part of our ancestral domain," Majaraj Julmuner Jannaral, Sultanate information officer, told the Philippine Star. "The Spratly Archipelago is part of the Sulu Sea (the inner area around the islands in the southern Philippines, which is part of the <a href="/wiki/West_Philippine_Sea" title="West Philippine Sea">West Philippine Sea</a> (designation of parts of the South China Sea claimed by the Philippines to be part of its EEZ)," Jannaral concluded. "Exploration of the marine territory and the waters around the Spratly Archipelago, Palawan in southwestern Philippines and the southern Philippines, belong to the residents in those areas," he added. The Sultanate of Sulu claims historic proprietary rights over the Spratly Islands since before the Spanish colonial era.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Various incidents of fishing boats being harassed by Chinese warships have occurred.<sup id="cite_ref-Archived_copy_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Archived_copy-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> At least 2 vessels were rammed or sunk. One was a Vietnamese ship attacked by a Chinese coastguard vessel, and another was a Fillipino ship rammed and sunk by a Chinese fishing boat who let the Fillipino fishermen drift at sea without giving aid. The stranded fishermen were later rescued by a Vietnamese ship.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2017, the United States despite not being a claimant in the Spratly dispute, reported using <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_Navigation_Operations" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom of Navigation Operations">freedom of navigation operations</a> (FONOPs) to challenge what it deemed as excessive and illegal maritime claims from multiple Asia-Pacific states including Cambodia, China, India, Indonesia, Malaysia, Maldives, the Philippines, Sri Lanka, Taiwan, and Vietnam.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2022, Vietnam demanded that Taiwan cease conducting <a href="/wiki/Live_fire_exercise" title="Live fire exercise">live fire exercises</a> at the Spratly Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2016_arbitration">2016 arbitration</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Philippines_v._China" title="Philippines v. China">Philippines v. China</a></div>
<p>In January 2013, the Philippines initiated arbitration proceedings against China under the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_on_the_Law_of_the_Sea" title="United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea">United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea</a> (UNCLOS) across a range of issues, including the latter's historic rights claims over parts or all of the Spratly Islands inside the <a href="/wiki/Nine-dash_line" title="Nine-dash line">nine-dash line</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-pressrelease_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pressrelease-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> A tribunal of arbitrators constituted under Annex VII of <a href="/wiki/UNCLOS" class="mw-redirect" title="UNCLOS">UNCLOS</a> appointed the <a href="/wiki/Permanent_Court_of_Arbitration" title="Permanent Court of Arbitration">Permanent Court of Arbitration</a> (PCA) as registry to the proceedings.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 12 July 2016, the arbitral tribunal ruled in favor of the Philippines on most of its submissions. While it would not "rule on any question of sovereignty over land territory and would not delimit any maritime boundary between the Parties", it concluded that China had not historically exercised exclusive control within the nine-dash line, hence has "no legal basis" to claim "historic rights" to resources.<sup id="cite_ref-pressrelease_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pressrelease-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> It also concluded that China's historic rights claims over the maritime areas (as opposed to land masses and territorial waters) inside the nine-dash line would have no lawful effect outside of what is entitled to under UNCLOS.<sup id="cite_ref-PCA_Award_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PCA_Award-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> It criticized China's land reclamation projects and construction of artificial islands in the Spratly Islands, saying that they had caused "severe harm to the coral reef environment".<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Finally, it characterized <a href="/wiki/Taiping_Island" title="Taiping Island">Taiping Island</a> and other features of the Spratly Islands as "rocks" under UNCLOS, and therefore are not entitled to a 200 nautical mile <a href="/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone" title="Exclusive economic zone">exclusive economic zone</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> The award was ruled as final and non-appealable by either country.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> China rejected the ruling, calling it "ill-founded".<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_2016_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_2016-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Taiwan, which currently administers Taiping Island, the largest of the Spratly Islands, also rejected the ruling.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Eight governments have publicly called for the ruling to be respected, 35 have issued generally positive statements without calling for parties to abide by it, and eight have publicly rejected it. The eight governments in support were <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>, Japan, <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>, the Philippines, <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a>, and the United States; the eight in opposition were China, <a href="/wiki/Montenegro" title="Montenegro">Montenegro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pakistan" title="Pakistan">Pakistan</a>, Russia, <a href="/wiki/Sudan" title="Sudan">Sudan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>, Taiwan, and <a href="/wiki/Vanuatu" title="Vanuatu">Vanuatu</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> itself does not have a position on the legal and procedural merits of the case or on the disputed claims, and the Secretary-General expressed his hope that the continued consultations on a Code of Conduct between <a href="/wiki/ASEAN" title="ASEAN">ASEAN</a> and China under the framework of the Declaration of the Conduct of Parties in the South China Sea will lead to increased mutual understanding among all the parties.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Transportation_and_communication">Transportation and communication</span></h2>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Telecommunications">Telecommunications</span></h3>
<p>In 2005, a cellular phone base station was erected by the Philippines' <a href="/wiki/Smart_Communications" title="Smart Communications">Smart Communications</a> on <a href="/wiki/Pag-asa_Island" class="mw-redirect" title="Pag-asa Island">Pag-asa Island</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Vietnam Military Telecommunications Corp., known commonly as <a href="/wiki/Viettel" title="Viettel">Viettel</a>, established mobile coverage in the Spratly Islands in 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 18 May 2011, <a href="/wiki/China_Mobile" title="China Mobile">China Mobile</a> announced that its mobile phone coverage has expanded to the Spratly Islands. The extended coverage would allow soldiers stationed on the islands, fishermen, and merchant vessels within the area to use mobile services, and can also provide assistance during storms and sea rescues. The service network deployment over the islands took nearly one year.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

"Asian Nations Squabble Over Obscure String of Islands". <i>Los Angeles Times</i>. p.&#160;A2.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Los+Angeles+Times&amp;rft.atitle=Asian+Nations+Squabble+Over+Obscure+String+of+Islands&amp;rft.pages=A2&amp;rft.date=1992-02-02&amp;rft.aulast=Whiting&amp;rft.aufirst=Kenneth&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASpratly+Islands" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-Brunei-70"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Brunei_70-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20080228205656/http://www.icriforum.org/secretariat/press_061303.html">Borneo Post: When All Else Fails</a>(archived from <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.icriforum.org/secretariat/press_061303.html">the original</a> on 28 February 2008) Additionally, pages 48 and 51 of "The Brunei-Malaysia Dispute over Territorial and Maritime Claims in International Law" by R. Haller-Trost, Clive Schofield, and Martin Pratt, published by the <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.dur.ac.uk/ibru/">International Boundaries Research Unit</a> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20091018082015/http://www.dur.ac.uk/ibru/">Archived</a> 18 October 2009 at the <a href="/wiki/Wayback_Machine" title="Wayback Machine">Wayback Machine</a>, University of Durham, UK, points out that this is, in fact, a "territorial dispute" between Brunei and other claimants over the ownership of one above-water feature (Louisa Reef)</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-146"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-146">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The <a href="/wiki/Taiping_Island_Airport" title="Taiping Island Airport">Taiping Island Airport</a> was completed in December 2007, (<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.taipeitimes.com/News/taiwan/archives/2006/01/06/2003287638">"MND admits strategic value of Spratly airstrip."</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Taipei_Times" title="Taipei Times">Taipei Times</a></i>. 6 January 2006. p. 2 <i>(MND is the ROC Ministry of National Defense)</i>), and a <a href="/wiki/C-130_Hercules" class="mw-redirect" title="C-130 Hercules">C-130 Hercules</a> transporter airplane first landed on the island on 21 January 2008.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-147"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-147">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFBong_Lozada2014" class="citation web cs1">Bong Lozada (18 June 2014). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://globalnation.inquirer.net/106698/air-force-to-repair-pagasa-island-airstrip/">"Air Force to repair Pagasa Island airstrip"</a>. <a href="/wiki/Philippine_Daily_Inquirer" title="Philippine Daily Inquirer">Philippine Daily Inquirer</a><span class="reference-accessdate">.
